Long-term memory for an AI companion without a vector database: 40 messages verbatim + 15 distilled facts

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I run Lovora, an AI companion service for adults in nine languages. The number one thing people ask an AI companion is "will she remember me?", and "memory" turns out to mean two different things. Here is the design we use, and why.

1. The recent conversation, word for word.

On every reply the model receives the last 40 messages of the chat, both sides, exactly as written. In voice calls it is the last 24, because spoken turns need to be answered faster and the prompt has to stay small.

This layer is what keeps a scene or an argument coherent. A summary would lose the exact wording, the jokes and the thing she promised two messages ago, which is exactly what people notice in a conversation.

Every 5 messages the user sends, a separate pass reads the latest part of the conversation and updates a short list of lasting facts: at most 15 short facts, about 1,500 characters. That list goes into the prompt of every later reply, in chat and in calls.

That is the layer that lets her ask about the job interview you mentioned last week, hundreds of messages later.

The distillation pass is instructed to keep facts about the user, never about the character, whose personality and backstory are fixed:

Small talk is discarded. A fact that is contradicted later is updated or dropped, not appended: the list is rewritten, not grown. The character is told to use what she remembers naturally, by asking about it or referring to it, and never to recite it as a list. Reciting is the fastest way to make a companion feel like a database.

We chose not to retrieve past messages by similarity. For a companion it has three problems:

A small fixed budget forces the distillation step to decide what matters, which is exactly the judgment retrieval skips.

The verbatim window is the expensive part: 40 messages is a real share of the context on every reply. The fact pass is cheap: it runs once every 5 user messages, as a separate call on a short excerpt.

If you are building anything conversational (support bots, tutors, companions), I would start here: a verbatim window sized to your latency budget, plus a small, rewritten list of facts about the user. Retrieval can come later if you ever need it.

The full, current description is in our docs: How memory works on Lovora.

In a Midtown Office, Artists Sift Through the Junkspace of Capitalism

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An intergenerational group of artists confronts technology as both a pathogen and a cure for today’s cultural ailments in the husk of an office at 260 Madison.

A few hours before 260 Madison opened on September 24, I trekked to its namesake address in Midtown to meet the curators. A security guard directed me to the 14th floor of the 1950s office building, where drop ceilings, an exposed HVAC system, and two people I assumed were the exhibition organizers dressed in corporate drag greeted me. They quickly clarified they were not; they were from AmTrustRE, the real estate company that offered the derelict 23,000-square-foot office space to the artists for free while preparing it — and three other unoccupied floors — for their next corporate tenants.

The three artists-turned-curators, Shaina Tabak, Dayana Matesheva, and Edison Niebla Rodríguez (the latter two of whom make up the duo DemonLovers Inc.), organized 260 Madison as an intergenerational, “inter-everything” show with work by over 50 artists, including self-taught and non-traditionally educated practitioners. On view through this Sunday, November 22, the show arrives just as the Whitney Museum of American Art opens artport: A History of Internet Art, signaling a new wave of institutional recognition for internet-grown art. With 260 Madison artists Maya Man, Rachel Rossin, and Eva and Franco Mattes among those included in the Whitney’s 25th-anniversary exhibition for its net art commissioning platform, the Midtown show feels especially urgent, if only because it takes place at the precipice of a paradigmatic shift within the art world.

Upon entering the first room on the left, I was immediately met with artist Jon Kessler’s towering nine-foot-tall sculpture, in which four plastic male dolls in Adidas tracksuits dangled from a weighted contraption somewhere between a carnival ride and a torture device. Titled “It Takes a Global Village Idiot” (2018) after philosopher Marshall McLuhan’s early ’60s theory that information-age technology “recreates the world in the image of a global village,” the sculpture forces viewers to confront the fact that the quick fixes of amusement proffered by technology rely on the same methods of data extraction that undergird systemic violence. It’s an unsettling introduction to a show where, even in its more humorous, delusionally conspiratorial, or enchanting moments, discomfort is always in the periphery.

“We don’t want to make a show or art that tries to be some form of escapism,” Rogríguez told Hyperallergic during an interview the week after the opening. “It’s like the [Werner Herzog] quote, ‘The poet must not avert his eyes.’ If some art in the show seems depressing, it’s because we live in what seems a depressing time.”

Rodríguez and Matasheva, who are also a couple, began making work together seven years ago while studying film in Montreal. Their video-art duo is named after Olivier Assayas’s 2002 film Demonlover, where multiple global corporations compete for a new immersive Japanese hentai company through extreme methods of espionage, exploitation, and blackmail.

“DemonLovers is about the libidinal economy and how algorithms are weaponizing our very basic instincts and desires,” Matasheva told Hyperallergic, echoing Rodríguez’s interest in what he calls “financial Freud,” or the psychoanalytic dimension of digital capital flows.

Both DemonLovers’s own two works in the show and those they curated certainly don’t shy away from the degradation associated with extractive economies, be it of the self, physical infrastructure, or the proverbial global village. Nearby Kessler’s work, Theresa Tomi Faison’s “The Long Tail” (2026) climbs up one of the floor’s load-bearing columns. Across the curved sculpture’s six screens, images of memes, forum screenshots, and phrases ruminating on contemporary capital flow all accumulate and dissolve downstream. The river of digital spam ends at the viewer’s feet, inculcating the audience, but also the space itself, which the curators characterize as shaped by "junkspace,” which architect Rem Koolhaas describes as the fallout that “remains after modernization has run its course.”

Among works that are either readymades or play with the form’s history — including Anthony’s Discenza’s windowsill pile of unopened credit card and loan offer letters in “Offers” (2026) and Eva and Franco Mattes’s distended taxidermied cat, “Longcat” (2026) — the corporate detritus can be difficult to parse from the artistic interventions. At one point, I mistook a few square feet of wallpaper with the Champion logo for a new work. The curators clarified during a later conversation that it was actually kept from the office’s previous tenants, HanesBrands, which owns Champion.

Having also inherited the space’s trove of gutted bathrooms, broken drywall, and stripped concrete floors (in short, its junkspace), the curators said they view 260 Madison through “layers of site responsiveness” more than anything. With some works, that connection emerged during the eleventh hour of installation. With others, the site occasioned the work itself. Ivana Dama used exposed air vents for “The Air Was Already Here” (2026), a sound work that channels air through reeds, transforming the invisible, inaudible rhythms of the space into what the artist describes as a “sculptural presence.” Pictures Generation artist Matt Mullican took a 47-foot span of unused wall space as an opportunity to reflect on his five-decade practice of constructing, deconstructing, and classifying systems of representation and perception with largely nonverbal symbols.

“He spent three days here drawing his entire practice on the wall, right from the beginning to the end,” Matasheva said regarding Mullicant’s monumental graphite piece, “Representing The Work” (2026). “He said he will give a lecture about it and that it's a very important work for him. It's kind of a conclusion of his practice.”

Meanwhile, the show’s video works are lodged into broken columns, ceiling alcoves, liminal backrooms, bathroom plumbing, abstract paintings, and even a hanging pink punching bag, a veritable reunion of the last decade of post-internet art movements. Alumni from Joshua Citarella’s publishing platform Do Not Research, the New Museum-affiliated Rhizome, and the traveling video and film screening series Open Secret, including Faison, Dana Dawud, Yehwan Song, and Ruba Al-Sweel, have contributed works. Many of them feature themes of surveillance, internet-induced conspiracy, and social media performance, as well as the technological, often AI-abetted methods of production that have come to define such enclaves.

Yet, for all the immateriality and speculative knowledge that flows through these works, 260 Madison equally plumbs an idea of technology with a long, craft-driven history. Tabak, a sculptor with a background in furniture design, is the driving force behind this facet of the show — and the currents of divination, enchantment, and even sublimity that come with it.

“My practice is very handmade and material-focused, so I always know that that’s what I would contribute to the curatorial vision,” Tabak told Hyperallergic. “And just being in Midtown, you shouldn’t separate a show from what’s happening around it.

Tabak was particularly interested in curating works that “blur the line between the hand and technology,” inviting artists who “haven’t had a lot of chances to show in art contexts, whether it’s because they are in the design world or have nine-to-fives” to participate in the exhibition. She explains that many of them complicate the very notion of technology and its use, citing Brian Oakes’s “Vessel 9” (2025), which gathers 3D-modeled circuit boards into a suspended “techno-Gothic” chandelier, and Ryan Decker’s “The Wavemaker Falters” (2026), which uses a motorized, rudder-like instrument to swoosh water through a raised vessel resembling a spaceship.

“We view technology not as something that has arrived in the 20th century, but something that has made humanity possible,” Matasheva said, expanding on Tabak’s comment. “Language is a technology, a chisel is a technology, everything is a technology.”

While many of the show’s craft-driven works conjure the anthropologist Alfred Gell’s idea that art is a technology that enchants, and in turn, becomes enchanted, others wield technology in the service of care. Kylie Mitchell repurposes a light-seeking solar robot and discarded electronic parts to move a found bird’s nest and egg toward a life-sustaining heat lamp. A few dozen feet away, Gregory Kalliche’s installation “Black-tailed gull nest; Ring-necked Pheasant Nest; Mallard Nest” (2016) suspends an LED light over three metal folding chairs printed with images of bird egg nests. Ben Gould’s “Ark II” (2026), on the other hand, augments a hospital gurney with eight aluminum armatures that hold fresh red roses inside coil-shaped glass vials. Doubling as a literal manifestation of Shakespeare’s “this mortal coil” phrase in Hamlet, the sculpture’s sterile infrastructure butts against the transitory visual comfort proffered by its flowers, mirroring a healthcare ecosystem wherein privatization, optimization, and tech automation have whittled medicine’s promises of care, healing, and empathy into mere aesthetic veneers.

It’s no coincidence that many of the works in 260 Madison position technology as both a pathogen and cure for today’s cultural ailments. In fact, when the real estate developers initially contacted Columbia University’s School of the Arts, it was to invite artists to “activate” a 36,000-square-foot gutted Duane Reade on the building’s ground floor. The developers eventually retracted their offer and moved the exhibition to the 14th floor, citing ongoing construction. Meanwhile, the original plan for 260 Madison to occupy the husk of the Duane Reade will be realized in a different form next month. Now serving as the building’s new temporary lobby, the former pharmacy will play host to a different group exhibition curated independently by Tabak. Opening December 3, HOW I FEEL will feature artists working across sculpture, social architecture, photography, painting, performance, and programming, including Jessica Vaughn, Quay Quinn Wolf, André Magaña, and 260 Madison’s Grayson Cox and Luke Murphy. How the forthcoming Duane Reade show contends with this moment of tech-induced economic entropy, so intense that even an entity as extractive as a real-estate company cannot commit to its own speculations, remains to be seen.

How to Run Long-Running AI Agents in Production (2026): Runtime Limits, Durable State, and Surviving Restarts and Outages

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at controlplane.com • AI-assisted

Long-running AI agents need three things that serverless request handlers do not give them: a process with no short execution cap, state checkpointed outside the process so a restart resumes from the last step, and presence in more than one location so a provider outage does not stop the agent. Most teams find these gaps after a prototype works in development. In production it dies mid-run when a function times out at 15 minutes, a deploy kills the container, or a zone goes offline.

Disclosure: Control Plane publishes this guide.

Long-running AI agents fail in production when the infrastructure treats them like short-lived request handlers.

Production agents need a runtime that does not stop them after a few minutes, a state layer that survives process death, and enough redundancy to stay up through infrastructure failures.

Execution limits vary widely, and hitting a cap mid-run loses work unless a durable layer sits on top.

Short-lived serverless functions need a durable execution layer on top, and long-running container runtimes still need graceful shutdown and external state.

The right state layer depends on your existing infrastructure, how complex the workflow is, and how long the agent needs to pause.

A team that already runs Postgres can add durability with DBOS or the LangGraph Postgres checkpointer and little new infrastructure. Complex multi-day orchestration with many branches fits Temporal. The OpenAI Agents SDK documents integrations with Temporal, Restate, DBOS, and Dapr for long-running agents, and the OpenAI Responses API background mode runs long requests that are polled or delivered by webhook. A single cloud’s durable service keeps agent state inside that provider’s failure domain, so a provider outage stops the workflow along with the agent.

Eleven failure modes regularly stop production agents, and each has a control that prevents lost work.

Every control depends on state living outside the process and outside any single failure domain.

Graceful shutdown, rolling updates, multi-location replicas, and checkpoint resume work together.

When a deploy, scale-in, or node drain happens, the orchestrator sends SIGTERM to the container. The agent should catch it, stop picking up new steps, and complete or checkpoint the current step. A preStop hook runs before SIGTERM, and its time counts against the termination grace period, which defaults to 30 seconds on Kubernetes.

Rolling updates keep capacity during deploys by replacing replicas in batches (on Kubernetes, by default no more than 25% unavailable at once) and waiting for each new replica to pass readiness probes. A PodDisruptionBudget limits how many replicas are down at once during voluntary disruptions such as node drains; it does not throttle a Deployment’s own rolling update.

Multi-location replicas protect against zone, region, and provider outages. If the agent runs in two locations and one fails, the other keeps serving. Checkpointed state must live in a store the surviving replicas can reach, such as a Postgres database replicated across regions. Asynchronous replication can lose the latest checkpoints on failover, so the resumed step must be safe to repeat, and leases belong on one strongly consistent primary at a time. When that primary’s location fails, promote a replica in a healthy location and advance the fencing epoch so writes from the old primary’s era are rejected.

Resuming from checkpoints ties these together. A replica in a healthy location loads the last checkpoint and continues. Steps whose completion was recorded are skipped, the interrupted step runs again, and idempotency keys let that retry run without duplicate side effects. Routing users to the nearest healthy region covers the traffic side.

Control Plane handles Day 2 operations for you, including autoscaling, built-in observability, and security.

It runs five workload types: serverless, standard, stateful, cron, and VM. On Control Plane, standard workloads are long-running processes, so an agent runs continuously with no execution cap. Stateful workloads give each replica a stable identity and its own persistent volume; a replica keeps the same identity (such as agent-0) when it is restarted or rescheduled, each replica is bound to its own volume from a volume set, and volume sets support snapshots. An inbound HTTP request times out after at most 600 seconds, or up to 3,600 seconds with the cpln/timeoutSecondsOverride workload tag, so run longer agent tasks as background work in the workload, with clients polling for status or receiving a callback.

A Global Virtual Cloud (GVC) is Control Plane’s named set of locations across AWS, GCP, Azure, and Oracle Cloud Infrastructure regions, plus your own Kubernetes clusters. A workload runs in every location of its GVC. Each workload’s endpoint routes clients to the nearest location where it passes readiness probes, so when one location fails, traffic reaches the healthy ones. In-flight work in the failed location resumes elsewhere only if its state was checkpointed to a store the other location can reach and its lease has expired; the fencing token on checkpoint writes keeps a cut-off replica from committing progress for the same run, and idempotency keys keep its side effects from repeating.

For that store, Control Plane’s Template Catalog includes PostgreSQL, PostgreSQL HA, and multi-location databases such as Postgres Multi-Location and pgEdge, which can back DBOS, the LangGraph Postgres checkpointer, or a custom checkpoint table; keep lease and fencing writes on a single primary, because multi-master replication resolves conflicts after the write. Temporal is also in the Template Catalog for teams that orchestrate with it. By default each replica gets a 90-second termination grace period, configurable in the workload’s rollout options. When a replica shuts down, Control Plane first removes it from load balancing, a default preStop hook then waits half the grace period (45 seconds at the default), and the container then receives SIGTERM, leaving the rest of the grace period for the agent to checkpoint. A queue-driven agent should also stop claiming runs when the preStop hook starts, since removal from load balancing only stops new HTTP requests.

Control Plane’s Universal Cloud Identity lets workloads call AWS, GCP, and Azure services with no long-lived cloud keys in your code. Control Plane Sandboxes run in Kata Containers microVMs and are persistent; they are for agents that build and run untrusted code, while the production agent runs as a workload. The Control Plane MCP server lets an agent operate infrastructure through the same Control Plane API as the CLI and console. Control Plane is PCI DSS Level 1, SOC 2 Type II, HIPAA, and GDPR compliant.

During the October 20, 2025 AWS us-east-1 outage, 20% of Control Plane customers had AWS us-east-1 in their GVC. Their workloads were active and running in healthy locations within 10 seconds, with no customer downtime, according to Control Plane’s internal incident telemetry.

Related reading: where to run AI agents in production, 10 AI agent infrastructure platforms compared, the cost to run AI agents in production, and letting AI agents operate production infrastructure safely.

These answers cover the questions engineering teams ask when they move AI agents from prototype to production.

Run them as long-lived processes on a runtime with no execution cap, checkpoint state to a durable store after every meaningful step, make side-effecting tool calls idempotent, handle SIGTERM during deploys and scale-in, retry model and tool errors with backoff, and run replicas in two or more locations.

Only with a durable layer on top, because a single Lambda invocation stops at 15 minutes. AWS Lambda durable functions extend runs to up to 1 year by checkpointing progress and replaying after failures, and AWS Step Functions Standard workflows also run up to 1 year.

Checkpoint the agent’s state to an external durable store after each completed step, then load the last checkpoint when the process restarts. Steps recorded as complete are skipped, and idempotency keys let the interrupted step run again without duplicate effects.

Durable execution persists a workflow’s progress so that after a crash, restart, or deploy, it resumes from the last completed step. Temporal rebuilds state by replaying event history, Inngest treats each step as a checkpointed unit of work, and DBOS checkpoints workflows and steps to Postgres.

In a durable external store that survives restarts and that every location running the agent can reach. Postgres is common, used directly by DBOS and the LangGraph Postgres checkpointer, while Temporal keeps state in its own persistence layer.

They run replicas in locations on more than one cloud provider and keep checkpointed state in a store reachable from all of them. When one provider fails, traffic moves to healthy locations, and a replica there claims the run, loads the last checkpoint, and continues. On Control Plane, a Global Virtual Cloud spanning AWS, GCP, Azure, and Oracle Cloud Infrastructure locations routes clients to healthy locations automatically.

No. Temporal suits complex multi-day orchestration, and Inngest, Restate, DBOS, AWS Step Functions, Azure Durable Functions, and Cloudflare Workflows also provide durable execution with different trade-offs. A simple workflow can also use custom checkpoint logic on Postgres.

As standard or stateful workloads with no execution cap, running in every location of a Global Virtual Cloud across AWS, GCP, Azure, and Oracle Cloud Infrastructure. Each workload’s endpoint routes to the nearest location that passes readiness probes, stateful replicas keep persistent volumes with snapshots, and a 90-second default grace period, configurable per workload, lets in-flight work drain and gives the agent time after SIGTERM to checkpoint.

Agent memory is the context an agent reasons with, such as conversation history and preferences, while durable execution state records which steps finished, what they returned, and what runs next. Memory can be summarized over time; execution state must be exact so a crashed agent resumes without repeating or losing work.

A rolling deploy replaces replicas in batches, and each old replica gets SIGTERM and a limited grace period. An agent that checkpoints on SIGTERM and releases its run continues on another replica from the last checkpoint; one that ignores SIGTERM loses its in-flight work.

Talk to the Control Plane team to run long-running AI agents across clouds, or sign up free to start building.

Originally published on the Control Plane blog.

Bitcoin miners face a new profitability cliff as difficulty heads for an 8.5% jump

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin miners face shrinking revenue and tighter electricity margins as a projected difficulty increase threatens less efficient machines.

Bitcoin miners’ expected dollar revenue for the same computing power fell about 6% from Nov. 16 to Nov. 20 in two readings of Luxor’s revenue index.

A difficulty cut had lifted expected Bitcoin earnings earlier in the month, but with the next adjustment now tracking higher, less efficient machines could soon need cheaper electricity to cover their power bills.

Simple Mining’s Nov. 20 reading of Luxor’s hash price index put gross mining revenue at $28.58 per petahash per second (PH/s) per day, with Luxor’s Nov. 16 roundup listing $30.45.

To estimate a machine’s gross daily revenue, multiply the hash price by the machine’s computing power in PH/s. One PH/s equals 1,000 terahashes per second.

The slide also appeared in CloudMineCrypto’s daily series, which estimates revenue from Bitcoin blocks. In its partial Nov. 20 reading, dollar hash price had fallen to about $28.90 from $30.30 per PH/s per day on Nov. 16, while the coin rate held near 0.00047 BTC.

The paired readings explain why a miner can earn roughly the same amount of Bitcoin for a day of work and still have fewer dollars to pay an electricity bill.

Bitcoin’s Nov. 14 adjustment lowered difficulty by 9.13%, from 148.36 trillion to 134.82 trillion. Holding the reward per block steady, the same amount of work could then earn about 10% more Bitcoin. Luxor’s Nov. 16 roundup found that the BTC hash price had risen from 0.00042763 a week earlier to 0.00046839 BTC per PH/s per day.

Over the same period, the dollar hash price fell from $31.95 to $30.45.

The expected coin payout depends on the block subsidy, transaction fees, and how hard it is to find a block. The dollar payout also depends on Bitcoin’s price, since fees accounted for only a small fraction of block rewards in both data sets.

Luxor reported a weekly average of 0.0229 BTC in fees per block on Nov. 16, and the partial Nov. 20 data imply about 0.022 BTC in fees on top of the 3.125 BTC subsidy.

As of Nov. 20, MineOrNot counted 1,008 blocks, halfway to the next adjustment. Blocks were arriving about every nine minutes and 14 seconds, compared with Bitcoin’s ten-minute target.

If that pace persists, MineOrNot estimated difficulty would rise 8.47% to about 146.24 trillion around Nov. 27.

If Bitcoin’s price, the subsidy, and average fees per block stay fixed, an 8.47% rise in difficulty would lower the $28.58 benchmark to about $26.35 per PH/s per day. A 10% increase would put it near $25.98 and bring difficulty almost back to its pre-cut level.

For a machine using 30 joules per terahash, the observed benchmark translates to a power-only break-even rate of 3.97 cents per kilowatt-hour.

With an 8.47% increase in difficulty, the same machine’s ceiling would fall to 3.66 cents. A machine using 13.5 joules per terahash can absorb higher electricity rates, with its ceiling falling from 8.82 cents to 8.13 cents under the same assumptions.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Gross mining revenue can cover a lower electricity rate if difficulty rises 8.47%, even with Bitcoin’s price and block rewards held fixed.

At a hypothetical 3.8¢/kWh, a 30 J/TH rig would stop covering its power bill if difficulty rises 8.47%. The 13.5 J/TH rig would still cover electricity.

Electricity-only model. Excludes pool fees, hosting, hardware and other costs.

Use the November 20 benchmark of $28.58 per PH/s per day. Model the retarget by dividing by 1.0847. At 1 PH/s, the 30 and 13.5 J/TH rigs draw 30 and 13.5 kW. Divide daily gross revenue by 24 times power in kW, multiply by 100, and round to 0.01 cent per kWh.

Electricity-only model. Excludes pool fees, hosting, hardware and other costs.

At a hypothetical electricity rate of 3.8 cents per kWh, the less efficient machine would just cover its power bill at the Nov. 20 benchmark. At that same rate, it would fall short after an 8.47% increase in difficulty. The more efficient machine would cover electricity at that rate in both cases.

The calculation divides gross daily revenue by daily electricity use. A miner at the ceiling has already used all its expected revenue on electricity. Pool fees and any separate hosting charges can lower the rate it can afford, and the operator’s contract and downtime will shape its actual costs.

As you can intuit from the headline, we've got good news and bad news regarding Valve's Steam Machine.

First, the good news: Valve announced that it's worked through all of the waitlist orders for the 512GB model of the Steam Machine, and you can now purchase one for as long as supplies last. This is related to the bad news, but it might take a while for Valve to fulfill 2 TB requests. There are more people on that list than there are consoles available.

Why Globant Stock Flew Higher on a Lousy Friday for the Market

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It posted an encouraging third-quarter earnings report.

By Eric Volkman – Nov 20, 2026 at 5:48PM EST

Globant (GLOB +4.13%) was one of the rare stocks to rise amid the sharp decline of the wider market on Friday. It acquired that port-in-a-storm quality on its latest set of quarterly results, which were good enough to draw investors to its equity. The digital transformation specialist's shares ended the day more than 4% higher.

Globant's third-quarter revenue was $611.1 million, down 1% year over year. Non-IFRS (adjusted) net income also declined, falling by almost 9% to nearly $63.8 million, or $1.48 per share.

That earnings per share (EPS) figure met analyst expectations while revenue notched a slight beat. The pundit top-line consensus for that line item was $610.9 million.

Like the more progressive legacy software companies, Globant -- which specializes in developing custom software for enterprises automating aspects of their business -- has embraced artificial intelligence (AI) in its solutions. It said in its earnings release that its recently introduced AI Pods subscription service had doubled its annualized run rate.

Globant quoted co-founder and CEO Martin Migoya as saying that "We are seeing growing enterprise adoption as clients shift from hourly billing toward outcome- and consumption-based delivery."

It's one thing to embrace AI as either a tool for internal software development or to tack on some of its functionalities to an existing software suite. It's quite another to lead with it as a product offering, like Globant is doing with AI Pods.

The service is clearly resonating with the market, and I think it might soon become a driver of growth for this company given its relatively high margins. This is starting to look something like a sleeper stock among the many software titles on the market, and as such, it's well worth watching.

The Build Passed. He Still Owes the Explanation.

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A finished check means the argument typechecks. It does not mean you understand it.

Levent Alpöge says he and Claude formalized a proof of the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis, without assumptions. He also says he has not mastered the mathematics sitting around that argument.

He says the formalization is finished. Turning it into a huge document with sufficient detail is, in his account, routine. He amused himself and got past 250,000 pages. Claude called the pile a book.

Theorem 1 opens by noting that the real numbers are totally ordered. If a number is not one half, it is bigger, or it is smaller. A checker will spend a page saying so. That is how you know a human never edited it for a reader.

He did not post the pile. He says posting that unreadable dump would be inappropriate. He published the Lean instead, in fourteen parts.

He is showing a complete argument before he has mastered a large neighborhood of it. The files are ahead of the author.

Where Claude did the technical work, his timeline is a paraphrase of a summary he warns may make no sense. He points at the Lean. He says he is too inexpert there to explain it himself.

His instinct is that the argument will get much simpler, and soon. He has not had time to find that shorter version. He calls this one step. The unpaid work is to understand it, simplify it, talk about it, and write it properly.

Same trap as a generated patch you cannot walk someone through. The green run is not the tour.

The Lean can check out while you are still late to your own result.

A green build is permission to start explaining. It is not permission to stop.

GTA 6 Metacritic User Scores Go Live, Resulting In Mass Review Bombing

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After an incredibly long wait, Grand Theft Auto 6 has dropped to massively positive reviews from critics, who have repeatedly described Rockstar's latest release as one of, if not the best games ever made. While there was plenty of debate over whether the critic reviews and player responses would actually line up or not when it released, the public response has been almost equally impressive so far.

Despite taking the industry by storm and boasting sales that broke a new record for the entertainment industry, GTA 6's review rating has just plummeted on Metacritic immediately after the site opened its user scores.

As noted by users like CharlieIntel on X, while there are plenty of user reviews that note the flaws while celebrating its achievements, the User Review section for GTA 6 on Metacritic has been absolutely flooded with overwhelmingly negative reviews, many of which are from players who admit they "haven't even played the game" themselves.

With GTA 6 currently sitting at a 5.1 user score at the time of writing, over half of the reviews uploaded for the game are negative, with countless politically charged reviews calling it "woke slop" and a "cuck simulator" over its relationship gameplay mechanics, or users straight-up keyboard-smashing negative reviews just so they can contribute to tearing down the game's score.

While it isn't exactly surprising that one of the most widely talked about games in history would wind up being the target of annoyed players or internet trolls wanting to join in on the fun, Take-Two had already expressed their concern about this exact situation happening years before GTA 6 released.

Even Nintendo's recently released Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time Remake wasn't exempt from the same fate either, with Link's return being slammed by players in response to the game's popularity, rather than the actual quality of the remake.

Considering that there are some genuine complaints to be had surrounding GTA 6's console performance, control scheme, or returning franchise features that are currently absent in the newest release, it's hard to pinpoint what the actual score will end up being after the dust of its release settles.

A new survey suggests that most people don't consider it cheating, yet the BBC ran with it anyway.

If the critic scores and the Metacritic rating for Rockstar's previous games are anything to go by, however, Grand Theft Auto 6 will likely wind up with an incredibly high score regardless of the current review bomb storm, especially as the game gets even bigger with the eventual PC port and multiplayer release.

How to fix "MCP: Unsupported protocol version"

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at runheed.com

Returned with HTTP 400 (code -32000) by HTTP MCP servers built on @modelcontextprotocol/sdk 1.x when a request's MCP-Protocol-Version header names a version the SDK does not know. SDK 1.32.2, the last 1.x release, supports 2025-11-25, 2025-06-18, 2025-03-26, 2024-11-05 and 2024-10-07. MCP 2026-07-28 is the first stateless revision: it drops the initialize handshake, so a client speaking it sends the version on every request, and the Python SDK 2.x lists it as its latest version.

If clients still connect, there is nothing to fix today: a client that supports both eras treats a 400 without a modern error body as a legacy server and falls back to initialize, which your 1.x server answers. To serve clients that speak only 2026-07-28, move the server to the v2 package (@modelcontextprotocol/server), which supports both. Do not fake the new protocol's UnsupportedProtocolVersionError (-32022) from a 1.x server: that error tells a client the server is modern, so it stops falling back.

Konstra AI keeps GPU clusters running around the clock, while partner Glows.ai's flexible compute and (k) ATP Token's usage governance extend the One KONST service chain

CHICAGO, Nov. 20, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- KONST, an AI compute services company rooted in Taiwan and expanding across Asia, is exhibiting at SC26 (Super Computing 2026), the International Conference for High Performance Computing, Networking, Storage, and Analysis, held November 15â20 at McCormick Place in Chicago (Booth 1062). Before Delivery: Validate First, Then Go LiveAt its booth, KONST is showcasing GPU cluster configurations ranging from NVIDIA H100 and H200 to B200 and B300. Before delivery, Konstra AI handles everything from architecture design and rack deployment through tuning, then validates each cluster with tests such as NCCL and HPL, so customers receive compute that is ready for training and inference from day one. This is the foundation of the service chain, providing dedicated AI compute for customers with long-term, large-scale training and inference needs.

After Delivery: Round-the-Clock OperationsIn Taiwan, Konstra AI already provides 24/7 remote and on-site operations for multiple large GPU clusters, both self-built and managed. Once each site is delivered, Konstra AI's professional team takes over round-the-clock operations: 24/7 monitoring of GPU health, network performance and power load; immediate incident response with root cause analysis (RCA) reports; continuous cluster tuning to raise compute utilization; and access management with two-factor authentication, per-site role-based permissions and an audit trail for every action. (k) ATP Token: Extending Operations to Model UsageAt the top of the service chain sits the output end of the Token Factory: Horizon AI's (k) ATP Token. Enterprises that want to use models directly can connect to more than 70 leading models through a single key, with prepaid billing and no monthly fee, keeping AI usage and budgets manageable and traceable. Horizon AI is an enterprise AI adoption partner and a member of the NVIDIA Inception program. From GPU clusters and flexible compute to model access, enterprises can start at any layer and expand as they scale, always working with a single point of contact.

"SC26 is where the global HPC community comes together, and we want international partners to see that they can find compute services in Asia that connect everything from GPU clusters and cloud platforms to model governance," said Ben Chang, co-founder and chairman of KONST. "Taiwan builds the world's AI hardware, and KONST's job is to turn that hardware strength into compute that all of Asia can use â and to keep it running reliably around the clock."

'Star Trek' Officially Has a New Home Base on Apple TV

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Feel free to connect with him or check out his work. He's everywhere — Upwork, YouTube, Spotify, SoundCloud, Collider, LinkedIn, Instagram.

The Apple TV Store currently looks less like a digital storefront and more like Starfleet Command, thanks to the ongoing discounted sale on the store. One particular franchise seems to be taking an exceptional lead during the sale, which proves that Trekkers are still solid even 6 decades later.

According to FlixPatrol's November 18 U.S. rankings, six of the platform's 10 most-popular television titles belong to Star Trek, while a seventh has cracked the Top 20. The sale went live around November 16 and includes titles like even Game of Thrones, Friends, The Sopranos, Supernatural, and The Big Bang Theory, among others. Most of these titles are cult favorites, but Star Trek still seems to have had a very strong breakthrough, which is what makes the dominance so impressive.

The whole Apple TV Store covers a large slice of Star Trek. For instance, The Original Series is $20 at the time of writing, instead of the original $60 price tag. The Animated Series is at $10 instead of $30. The Next Generation is at $40 instead of $100, while Star Trek: Enterprise, Star Trek: Picard, and Star Trek: Lower Decks are at $20 from their original $50 prices. There’s also Prodigy, which is at $15 (at a 50% discount) right now. In contrast, all eight seasons of Game of Thrones are currently available at $60 (original price $100), down 40% from its original price and still not making it to the Top 20 on Apple TV Store.

Paramount spent this year running a major anniversary campaign around six decades of the franchise, which now encompasses 11 television series, 14 files and more than 900 episodes. There were anniversary screenings, worldwide events, a Guinness World Records initiative around the Vulcan salute, merchandising collaborations and other celebrations throughout 2026. That whole advertising grind from Paramount’s end seems to have beautifully allowed the Apple TV Store to lock in on that traction.

Other than the ones already mentioned, there’s Star Trek: Deep Space Nine available on Apple TV Store for $35, but its shallower discount may help explain its lower #16 placement. Strange New Worlds is still an ongoing series sold season-by-season, which explains why it’s not on the list.

Star Trek is currently trending on the Apple TV Store and available at heavily discounted prices. Stay tuned to Collider for more updates.

APEC CEO Summit 2026 Concludes Successfully in Shenzhen

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SHENZHEN, China, Nov. 20, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- The APEC CEO Summit 2026 concluded on November 18 at the Qianhai International Convention Center in Shenzhen. Hosted by the China Council for the Promotion of International Trade (CCPIT), the summit was held from November 17 to 18 as a supporting event of the 33rd APEC Economic Leaders' Meeting. President Xi Jinping attended the opening ceremony and delivered a keynote speech. The summit drew more than 2,000 political and business leaders from all 21 APEC economies and 13 economies beyond APEC, including the United Kingdom, France, Germany and Switzerland.

Under the theme "To Prosper and Win the Future Together," the summit featured the opening ceremony, speeches by leaders of APEC economies, a business dialogue, thematic discussions, the closing ceremony and the handover of the hosting role. Leaders of APEC economies including Australia; Canada; Chile; Hong Kong, China; the Republic of Korea; Malaysia; Mexico; Russia; Thailand and Vietnam attended the summit, delivering speeches or remarks or joining panel discussions. More than 40 heads of leading Chinese and foreign companies and major business associations exchanged views on the Asia-Pacific economic outlook and new opportunities for cooperation, connectivity, artificial intelligence, sustainable development, the Free Trade Area of the Asia-Pacific (FTAAP), the digital economy, development of the Guangdong-Hong Kong-Macao Greater Bay Area, and digital and green transformation.

Ren Hongbin, Chairman of the CCPIT and Chair of the APEC CEO Summit 2026, presided over the opening ceremony. At the closing ceremony, on behalf of the Asia-Pacific business community, he released The Shenzhen Initiative of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) CEO Summit 2026, calling for deepening an open regional economy, driving inclusive growth through technological innovation, strengthening regional connectivity, and working together to achieve shared prosperity. It is the first time in the summit's 30-year history that outcomes have been issued in the form of an initiative.

At the handover ceremony, Ren Hongbin passed the hosting role for the next APEC CEO Summit to Nguyen Quang Vinh, Vice Chairman of the Vietnam Chamber of Commerce and Industry.

More information: https://www.apecceosummit2026.cn/en/apec2026/

Melania Trump Stresses Humanitarian Efforts at Chic Premiere for Two-Episode Series Documentary Follow-Up

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

At a tight-security screening at New York's Lincoln Center Thursday night, the first lady's longtime collaborators celebrated how the Brett Ratner-helmed show allows her to tell her own story.

In late January, Amazon‘s Brett Ratner-directed Melania documentary debuted at the Kennedy Center in Washington amid crowds of demonstrators in the wake of the fatal shootings by ICE agents of Alex Pretti and Renée Good in Minneapolis. The president had also sparked backlash for his efforts to overhaul the Kennedy Center, including adding his name to the exterior, which are still playing out in court.

Thursday night, Melania: The Series, a two-episode follow-up, though part of the initial $40 million project, consisting of bonus footage, debuted at New York’s Lincoln Center. Those involved with the film and series downplayed any significance of the change in venue, noting the president and first lady’s ties to New York. But if there were demonstrators, they weren’t seen or heard by those inside the tight-security screening at Alice Tully Hall, though whether that’s due to people moving on or the premiere location remains an open question. Related Stories Business Trump's $15 Billion Defamation Suit Against The New York Times Moves Forward TV CBS Inks New Deal to Air Kennedy Center Honors

The screening came just two weeks after the 2026 midterm elections saw Democrats score historic, surprise victories in a referendum on the president that will result in a change in party control of both the House of Representatives and the Senate.

Still, Trump’s longtime adviser Marc Beckman previously teased that the docuseries, now streaming on Amazon’s Prime Video, would be a “total different experience, completely new.”

Speaking to The Hollywood Reporter ahead of the Melania: The Series premiere, Beckman explained how the filmmaking team determined what would go in the documentary and what would be for the series.

“We decided that we would bifurcate and isolate certain stories that we thought were suitable for the series that weren’t necessarily appropriate for the film,” Beckman said. “The reason they’re suitable for the series is because it gives us more time to unpack more of her storyline, more about her family, more about her career, more about philanthropy and also what happens right after the inauguration when she takes on the role of being first lady of the United States again.”

Melania: The Series producer Fernando Sulichin, who took on the same role on the documentary, added that the film “was about going into the inauguration, and this one is first lady in action and what she’s about, more intimate — not the preparation of becoming the first lady but being the first lady.”

Beckman likened the approach to what Trump’s team did with her 2024 self-titled memoir Melania.

“With the trade edition of the book, we tell the story and we have certain photos in it, and then the collector’s edition, it’s a completely different experience so the fans could get both and enjoy them,” he said. “That’s the same thing here.”

Though it was initially expected that the docuseries would arrive on Amazon’s Prime Video when the documentary began streaming in March, Beckman said that was “just the media speculating,” and insisted the timing of the series’ release was in keeping with the licensing deal with Amazon he negotiated for the film and series.

“The timing for the contract is exactly what we’ve done,” he told THR on the black carpet Thursday night. “I negotiated the contract, so trust me, there’s no delay.”

While the documentary film, which was released in theaters in late January, followed Trump in the two weeks leading up to the start of her husband’s second term as president, the docuseries, in which Trump answers softball questions about her daily routine, interests and beliefs, first focuses on her role as a wife and mother, including her relationship with the president, followed by an episode centered around her and the president’s trips to North Carolina in the wake of Hurricane Helene and Los Angeles after the 2025 wildfires.

The film, coming in the wake of Trump’s best-selling memoir, was seen as part of a launch of a lifestyle brand. The doc and series’ $40 million Amazon purchase sparked backlash that the tech giant was trying to curry favor with the White House, criticism likely to continue with the project’s latest extension.

And while the Lincoln Center screening boasted Trump’s signature black-and-white color palette, complete with a black carpet and white tables and flowers, as well as tickets and popcorn boxes featuring the first lady’s face, Beckman steered questions about a possible lifestyle company back to Trump’s humanitarian efforts, specifically her work brokering a ceasefire in Oleshky, Ukraine and her Fostering the Future initiative to provide resources and educational opportunities for foster kids.

“I think right now what she’s really proud of is how she’s helping children and people throughout the world,” Beckman said. “What she’s doing in America is so much more important than a lifestyle brand.”

And before the docuseries premiered, Trump spoke, from a black-and-white podium bearing her name, about the Ukraine ceasefire, connecting it to her own personal ethos and the series’ focus, and episode titles, of “Family & Trust” and “Pride & Honor.”

“We live in a world that never stops moving, but life’s greatest moments belong to those who can stand still,” she began. “In calmness, we can see beauty. In peace, we can see humanity. My work is demanding, and sometimes the challenges I face seem impossible. Still, even in the most difficult circumstances, I find the drive to achieve the impossible. I rely on my own strength and character, with conviction, courage and reckless determination. I found a way forward where others would not.”

“For a brief moment, the world stood still for Oleshky. Eight hundred lives were saved. After so many years of fighting, the ceasefire brought a little bit of sunshine into lives which had been in the shadows of fear and war. The moment was very powerful for me because fighting and conflict have no value to the families on either side. Peace is rational,” she continued. “In the Oleshky ceasefire, we see the power of family, the restoration of trust and the courage of young children who do not back down in the face of war. Family, trust, pride, honor are the values we all hold in our hearts; they are also the foundation of the personal story I’m going to share with you tonight as part of Melania: The Series.”

As Trump teased that the series offered the opportunity to “experience [her] world through a new lens,” Trump’s current and former colleagues said they hoped the show, like the documentary, let Trump tell her own story.

President Trump’s first-term adviser Kellyanne Conway said conceptually that the documentary and series were “so amazing.”

“She’s obviously a very private person who has a very public life and in my view a very positive impact and that story does not get told,” Conway told THR. “And that’s OK, so let her tell her own story.”

Fashion designer Hervé Pierre, who created the first lady’s inaugural gowns for the president’s first and second ceremonies, added, “She’s not mysterious, but she’s not all over the media all the time and I think when she does something like she just did with [Russia and Ukraine], it’s not, like, fluff. It’s a very specific mission. So I think it’s important. I hope we will discover something that we don’t know about her. And I think sometimes it’s better for her to [express something in her own words] than to have an interpretation of what she’s doing. At least you have a direct line.”

And what sort of things might viewers discover about Trump that they don’t already know?

Pierre, who’s long worked with the first lady, offered a couple of insights.

“She cries when she laughs,” he said of her tendency to tear up at humorous moments. “She’s very happy. I’ve worked with difficult women for 30 years in haute couture. She never, ever raised her voice or [had attitude]. She’s always in a good mood — it’s fascinating.”

French police officers sentenced for beating of music producer Michel Zecler

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The three officers were seen on video beating up music producer Michel Zecler in his studio

Three French police officers have been convicted of beating up a black music producer six years ago in Paris in what the court upheld as an aggravated circumstance of racism.

The three were also found guilty of signing a falsified police report stating that the producer - Michel Zecler, 47 - had violently resisted their attempt to arrest him.

Their lies were exposed because the whole scene at Zecler's recording studio was caught on CCTV, revealing the producer had suffered some 30 punches, kicks and baton strikes while offering little resistance at all.

Aurelien Lapersonne, 29, Philippe Tondeux, 50, and Pierre Plantier, 37, all received five year sentences, with different amounts suspended.

Lapersonne had three years of his sentence suspended and Tondeux three and a half years suspended. Plantier had four years suspended, and will serve his one year with an ankle monitor.

All were told they could never serve in the police again.

The case became a cause cÃ©lÃ¨bre for critics of the police, who say it exposed systematic racism among officers. With police again facing criticism for alleged use of excessive force in stopping recent protests, the verdict was hotly awaited by Zecler's supporters.

Much depended in the two-week trial on Zecler's own testimony that the officers called him a racial slur during the beating.

This was denied by the three policemen, who in court admitted the beating and the faked report but denied there was a racial motivation. But the court preferred the evidence of the producer, because - unlike the police - everything else he said was verifiably true.

Because the racist element was accepted by the court, the sentences were toughened.

The case was heard by a panel of five judges.

The incident took place during the Covid lockdown in 2020. Zecler was accused by the officers for not wearing a mask when doing so was obligatory as he approached his studio on foot.

They later claimed his behaviour aroused their suspicions, and they thought he might be carrying drugs. No evidence of this emerged.

After Zecler entered the lobby, the police followed - without a warrant - and tried to bring him outside. Zecler refused to go out, and the beating ensued. Zecler cried for help and some people appeared from the basement and pushed the police outside.

The officers then smashed a glass pane and sent a tear-gas grenade into the building, forcing those inside to come out - after which Zecler was again hit. He was then arrested.

During the trial Zecler said had it not been for the CCTV video, he would have been in court not as a victim but as a potential criminal.

In their last words before the court, all three police officers apologised for what they had done. Tondeux said he was sorry "for the image of the police that I have given".

GTA 6's map is so big it's going to take months for people to comb through it and find everything. Rockstar has already posted the full map of Leonida online, but it doesn't answer one simple question that I know we've all been wondering: How long would it take to fly across this entire thing in a plane?

The answer is a lot less time than it would take to go by car, but still long enough to give you an idea of the sheer scale we're working with here. Using the closest plane to Jason's house, my trip across Leonida took around 4 minutes and 18 seconds, which is a little under an hour of in-game time. You can watch the entire flight from start to finish below.

Keep in mind that the Mammoth Dodo seaplane that carried me from the southern tip of the archipelago to the northern tip of the map is just one type of plane in GTA 6. I would bet there are faster options if you really want to zoom.

Even though I'm still very early in the GTA 6's story, I didn't have to go too far out of my way to test this. Jason's house is conveniently located pretty close to a spot with perfectly unattended planes to borrow. The Sunbelt Highway that runs south of it will lead you right to the earliest planes and helicopters you can find. Just don't fly over the nearby military base (trust me, it's bad news) and you'll be fine.

Rockstar compared GTA 6's map to its previous games, describing it as about the size of GTA 4, GTA 5, and Red Dead Redemption 2 combined. Despite knowing this beforehand, I was still shocked to see how the map kept going when I first opened it up. Four minutes might not sound like a lot, but the enormity of it all sinks in as you glide above densely populated cities and neighborhoods that could be the setting of their own standalone videogames.

My experiment has left the fog of war on my map split into two halves. My flight path carved a line of partially revealed locations straight through it. I don't expect to clear the rest out for dozens of hours, but now I'm tempted to find out how long of a roadtrip it would be to do the same journey by car.

This Is What Was on the Menu at the First Thanksgiving in 1621

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Somehow, turkey has spent the last four centuries getting all the glory.

For a meal that has been discussed, depicted and reenacted for more than four centuries, the first Thanksgiving has left us surprisingly few details about what anyone actually ate. We know there were roughly 140 people, three days of feasting and exactly five deer. Beyond that, the historical record is rather unhelpful. Apparently, nobody thought to write down the menu.

The gathering took place in Plymouth in the fall of 1621, when about 50 English colonists celebrated their first successful harvest alongside 90 Wampanoag, including their leader, Ousamequin, better known to history as Massasoit. It was a meeting between two communities whose relationship was far more complicated than the cheerful scene that would eventually find its way into American classrooms. Although they had formed an alliance earlier that year, cultural differences and tensions of an English settlement on Wampanoag land were hardly going to disappear over dinner.

And while the English had much to celebrate in bringing in their first harvest, they owed a great deal of that success to Indigenous knowledge. The Wampanoag understood the region’s growing seasons, its waterways and the foods that could be grown and gathered in the surrounding landscape. Their traditions had helped sustain communities there long before the colonists arrived.

The two surviving English accounts of the gathering offer only a few details about the feast itself, but taken together with what historians know about the food traditions of both communities, they give us a rather interesting picture of what may have been on the table.

Edward Winslow, one of the English colonists who attended the gathering, recorded that the Wampanoag went hunting and returned with five deer, which they presented to the colony’s leaders. It’s one of the few specific foods mentioned in his account and an especially substantial contribution to a celebration that lasted three days.

Deer were an important source of food for the Wampanoag, who also made use of their hides, bones and other materials. In England, however, deer hunting was largely reserved for the wealthy, making venison something most ordinary people rarely got to enjoy. The meat may have been roasted over a fire or simmered in a hearty venison stew, although Winslow neglected to tell us how it was prepared. We know exactly how many deer arrived, but what became of them in the kitchen remains a mystery.

Roast turkey has become so thoroughly associated with Thanksgiving that it’s difficult to imagine the holiday without one occupying the center of the table. The trouble is, we don’t actually know whether turkey was served at the original celebration.

Winslow wrote that four Englishmen went hunting for wildfowl and returned with enough to feed the colonists for nearly a week. What he didn’t do was tell us which birds they shot. Wild turkeys were present in the region, but so were ducks, geese and other game birds, all perfectly suitable for roasting over a fire. There’s every possibility that turkey was on the menu, but nothing to suggest it enjoyed the distinction we’ve since bestowed upon it. For all we know, the centerpiece was a duck, and we’ve spent the last few centuries giving credit to the wrong bird.

Corn was one of the most important foods available to both communities, although the English were relatively new to growing it. The Wampanoag had cultivated maize for generations, developing agricultural practices suited to the region’s soil and growing seasons. Their knowledge was enormously valuable to the colonists, whose first attempts at establishing a food supply had been anything but fruitful.

One traditional Wampanoag preparation was nasaump, a thick porridge made from ground corn, sometimes cooked with other ingredients. Corn could also be ground into meal for simple breads, early relatives of the cornbread we bake today, or used to thicken stews and make other hearty dishes.

The waters around Plymouth were full of food, provided you knew where to look and how to catch it. The Wampanoag had long fished the region’s rivers and coastline, gathering shellfish and catching everything from bass and cod to eels. Clams, mussels and lobsters were plentiful too.

The English were beginning to take advantage of these resources by 1621. William Bradford, the Plymouth colony’s governor, wrote of the colonists catching substantial quantities of cod and bass during the summer. A freshly caught fish could be roasted or boiled, while clams and mussels might be cooked over a fire. Lobster was another plentiful local catch that was enjoyed regularly. The variety of ocean fare available might have made for a Thanksgiving spread that looked rather more like a seafood boil than the feast we know today.

Squash and pumpkin were important crops in Wampanoag agriculture, and their ability to keep well after harvest made them especially useful as the colder weather set in. Both could be roasted, boiled or added to stews, much like we still prepare winter squash today, though without the benefit of a balsamic glaze.

The English called pumpkins pompions, a name I think we abandoned prematurely. They were familiar with the vegetable, although the varieties growing in New England differed from those they might have seen at home. A pumpkin dish would have been entirely plausible at the harvest celebration, but anyone hoping for a slice of pumpkin pie would have to wait almost two centuries. The colonists had limited supplies of wheat flour, butter and sugar, and the familiar custard-filled pie wouldn’t appear in American cookbooks until 1796.

The Wampanoag grew beans alongside corn and squash, three crops that complemented each other remarkably well. Cornstalks supported climbing beans, while squash vines spread across the ground and helped shade the soil. The combination was as useful in the garden as it was on the plate.

A traditional Wampanoag stew called sobaheg combines ingredients like corn, beans, squash and meat or fish, depending on what’s available or in season. The combination of corn and beans also appears in succotash, another dish with Indigenous New England roots that remains an American favorite.

The forests and wetlands of New England offered plenty beyond cultivated crops. Chestnuts, walnuts, hazelnuts, acorns, wild grapes and cranberries were among the foods the Wampanoag knew how to gather and prepare, each with its own season and uses.

Nuts could be eaten plain, stored or ground into meal, while fruits could be enjoyed fresh or preserved for later. Acorns required particular attention, since their natural bitterness had to be leached out with water before they could be used in cooking. It’s one of the few ingredients we’ve more or less surrendered entirely to the squirrels.

Of all the wild fruits available, cranberries have perhaps the most recognizable place on a modern Thanksgiving table. Their natural tartness eventually found a friend in sugar, giving rise to the cranberry sauce that’s become nearly as much a part of the holiday as the turkey itself.

Kasper Hogh and the missed Celtic Christmas advert trick as Martin O’Neill weighs up penalty dilemma

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Celtic Christmas advert was released on Friday containing the usual good cheer and a little festive pop at Rangers. It did, though, miss an obvious storyboard opportunity.

All I want for Christmas is my first Parkhead goal. A sad-looking, shaggy-haired striker is seen traipsing through the snowy streets of the east end of Glasgow before bumping into a dreadlocked king of kings, who gives him the gift of putting the ball in the net.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Although Henrik Larrson is not involved, Kasper Hogh does in fact feature in the latest two-minute festive promo film, which has been released in conjunction with the Celtic FC Foundation’s Christmas appeal.

Callum McGregor calls out “Oi, youse two!” in the direction of Hogh and Benjamin Nygren. Viljami Sinisalo is more direct: “Kasper!” Nygren turns round, and says: “Who?”

It maybe doesn’t work as well when written down. Hogh – pronounced who - is clearly a good sport. However, as Martin O’Neill has said on a number of occasions, and repeated on Friday at Lennoxtown, at some point strikers need to start scoring goals.

Of course, Hogh thought he had finally bagged his first home goal in the hoops two weekends ago. Nygren set him up with a neat pass into the Dane’s path and seconds later the net billowed. Hogh celebrated with understandable gusto before the dismaying news that it was being ruled out for an innocuous foul in the build-up. “For him, that was a killer,” admitted O’Neill on the eve of the latest home game against St Mirren.

All eyes will be on the team sheet. Will Hogh - yet to score after eight Parkhead appearances - play? Having been dropped from the Danish squad for their most recent games against Portugal and Norway, the striker was given some time off in the recent international window.

“He has been here, we have given him some time….he has had no break at all because where he came from (Norway) they were playing matches throughout (the summer),” explained O’Neill.

The manager made an extraordinary – to use one his favourite words - admission following the recent game against Kilmarnock, when Nygren wasted a spot kick. He claimed he would not have let Hogh take the penalty, which visiting keeper Nico Mantl saved, if the Dane had been on the park at the time.

Despite the ever more urgent need for Hogh to break his duck at home, O’Neill clearly felt the potential mental harm from failing to convert from 12 yards would have been too great a risk. The game, with Celtic winning 1-0 at the time, was also on a knife edge. A pressure penalty might not have been what Hogh needed.

It will be interesting to see what happens against St Mirren if Celtic are awarded another penalty and Hogh is available to take it. One might expect the Dane to demand to be given the ball and put this ridiculous issue to bed. Adding to the intrigue is that Nygren, who has an Achilles knock picked up on international duty with Sweden, might not play. Not that O’Neill trusts him from the spot now in any case.

The manager was not a fan of Nygren’s penalty technique against Kilmarnock. “All I will say is that if Nygren plays, which he may or may not, he won’t take it!” he said. “All this (stuttered) walking up….why not just do an Alan Shearer and blast it into the net!? If you hit the goalkeeper, you’ll take his head off!”

Non-scoring strikers is a theme just now given Oli McBurnie’s continued struggles at international level for Scotland. After just being subbed on, one wondered whether Sebastien Pocognoli might have ordered him to take the penalty against North Macedonia at Hampden Park last week. Again, with the manager’s future prospects possibly resting on the kick, maybe not. John McGinn did the necessary and Pocognoli survives, for the time being.

Hogh won’t want any handouts. He’s a centre forward who thrives on goals. He will feel capable of working it out for himself. No charity, please.

As shown against Kilmarnock, he’s not far away. He missed one very good chance shortly after his chalked-off goal when one-on-one with Mantl. “If he had the first goal he would have taken that on another yard,” insisted O’Neill. “But that looked like a man in a real hurry to try and get his first (Celtic Park) goal.”

Easy going and charming, there are still limits to what Hogh can endure. He wouldn’t be human if it wasn’t having an effect.

“He seems philosophical about it at this minute,” O’Neill reported. “He thinks if he is working really hard for the side then he can get in….he should have taken a tip or two from Duran!”

Camilo Duran is the opposite. With 13 goals in 21 appearances, he has made a flying start to his Celtic career.

Or maybe the aforementioned Larsson can make a visit. Like Hogh, the Swede was not an immediate hit at Parkhead.

O’Neill himself suggested Larsson could have some input - “The best thing for me to do is bring Henrik Larsson over and show him how to hit the ball into the net!” - when the discussion turned to players who initially laboured under great expectations.

Perhaps the late Trevor Francis, his old teammate, was one too? O’Neill was at Nottingham Forest when the striker arrived at the City Ground to become Britain’s first £1 million player. Talk about pressure.

“He took a couple of weeks,” remembered O’Neill. “It didn’t help that he had just been signed for a million pounds and Brian Clough told him to make the tea for everyone, which he did!

“First match, he made the tea for us in the dressing room! That wasn’t exactly what Trevor was expecting.” Hogh, who cost eleven times what Clough paid for Francis, has not been made to suffer such indignities - yet.

What can AI learn from a thousand days of hearing what a baby hears?

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Princeton recorded eight babies for their first 1,000 days, and a model trained on one child's audio learned language on the same schedule.

A toddler can pick up hundreds of words just by hearing people talk. Could a computer start from scratch and recognize many of those same words on roughly the same timetable?

Researchers at the Princeton Neuroscience Institute wired up 15 homes with cameras and microphones and recorded babies for most of their waking hours across the first 1,000 days of life.

The researchers turned speech recorded around eight children into clean audio using synthetic voices.

They trained a separate model on each child’s audio in the order the speech was originally recorded.

The models identified recurring sound patterns that corresponded to words.

On average, each model reached the halfway point on a vocabulary checklist within 22 days of the child it was paired with.

“We wanted to turn the usual logic of developmental research around,” said Uri Hasson, a professor of psychology and neuroscience at Princeton who led the study.

“Instead of observing a child for an hour in the laboratory, we asked whether we could build a model capable of learning and immerse it in the child’s actual experience, day after day.”

The families were in New Jersey and spanned a range of incomes and backgrounds. Each home got four to 14 recording devices spread across two to five rooms.

The devices ran 12 to 14 hours a day, from February 2022 to May 2025.

The project gathered roughly 1.2 million hours of audio and video, of which the eight homes analyzed in this paper supplied about 600,000. An automated pipeline worked out where the child was in the house and pulled out the speech happening nearby.

Each child ended up with between 1,900 and 6,600 hours of usable speech, three to seven and a half hours a day. That adds up to 6 to 27 million words per child, over a median of 960 recording days.

Every month from eight months on, caregivers filled out a standard checklist of which words their child understood and said. Those forms are how the researchers knew what each child knew, and when.

Children reached half of the 649-word checklist anywhere from about 12 to 22 months old. That ten-month spread between children is the thing most studies average away.

The model started from random numbers. It had no dictionary, no phonetics, no notion of where one word ends and the next begins, and no text.

The program’s first part chops the incoming sound into 20-millisecond pieces and sorts them into categories it invents for itself. Its second part tries to guess the next piece from what came before.

Whenever a run of pieces becomes reliably predictable, the program files it away as a unit, which is the closest thing it has to a word.

Nothing about that design is specific to English or even to speech. What makes it unusual is the schedule.

The audio arrives in the order it was recorded, one day at a time, rather than shuffled into batches the way an AI language model is normally trained.

At the end of each simulated day, the program gets a sample of speech from earlier days, weighted toward recent ones. The researchers call it replay.

English has 39 phonemes, the sound units that distinguish bat from pat. Children spend their first year tuning in to the ones their own language uses.

Within roughly 12 to 15 months of simulated time, the program had carved out dedicated categories for nearly all of them.

Across the eight children, the models averaged 37.5 phonemes each. The lowest count came from the household with the least recording.

The order it happened in, sounds before words, is the order infants follow.

Words came after. For the child the paper calls Coral, the checklist said she understood 97 words at 12 months, and her model had 91.

Across all eight children, the models and the children hit the same vocabulary milestones within an average of 22 days of each other, with the worst case 56 days.

The models also reproduced the differences between children, landing close to each child’s own halfway age.

They did not match perfectly. The models tended to start learning words a little late and then catch up faster, so the shape of the curve was only partly right.

The surprise in the paper is what happened when the researchers took replay away.

Coral’s household produced something like 10 million words a year, which is a rich environment by any measure. Hearing all of it once, in order, wasn’t enough. With a single pass the program barely learned words at all.

Around 25 to 30 passes over each day’s material was enough, and more than that added very little.

The researchers link this to what sleep is thought to do for memory. A program replaying audio files is not a sleeping infant, and the paper is careful about that.

The amount of recording mattered just as much. Fed only a tenth of Coral’s daily speech, under an hour a day, the program learned none of the checklist words by 24 months.

At 30% of the day, about two and a half hours, it learned 80 of the 649 checklist words.

At 70% it got about nine-tenths of the way to the full result. The rarer words, it seems, only turn up for a listener who is there most of the day.

One limitation shapes everything else. Household recordings are full of clattering dishes, television, and two people talking at once, and the program was not asked to cope with any of that.

Instead the researchers transcribed all the speech around each child, then had a text-to-speech system read the transcripts back as clean audio.

Each household got a mix of eight adult female voices, with 30% of segments read in a voice matched to the child’s mother.

So the word order, the timing and the vocabulary are real, and the sound isn’t. The acoustic problem a baby actually solves, picking one voice out of a noisy room, was left for later work.

Three of the eight children also had shorter recordings, 14 to 20 months of usable input, so their later trajectories are extrapolated rather than modeled.

The program also sees nothing: no faces, no pointing, no object being handed over, which is a large part of how toddlers work out what a word refers to.

The program learned sounds and words. It did not learn grammar, and whether the same machinery would arrive at sentence structure is the open question the authors name.

The team wants to use the setup for questions nobody can put to a real baby. What would less sleep do, or screen speech instead of a parent’s?

Future studies could also add eye contact and the rest of the social world the cameras captured but the model never saw.

Hadas Raviv, a computational cognitive scientist in Princeton’s psychology department who built the analysis pipeline, framed the goal as the opposite of the usual one in artificial intelligence.

“Unlike much of modern AI, which aims for superhuman performance, we’re trying to understand how humans learn and how that learning gives rise to intelligence,” Raviv said.

The full study was published in the journal Science.
